ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e3391	Federal Chancellor, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e3407	(Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) At the moment, the Convention is meeting for the last time – today and tomorrow – and I believe that the Convention has done a great deal of work to reform the Constitution or to establish a Constitution for Europe. There are some very important key points that were once controversial today for all Member States – 25 Member States – the Union out of dispute: First of all, this is the whole question of the legal personality of the Union. If one can vote once, in ten or 20 years' time, with one vote in the UN Security Council, or if the Union will once appear to the international financial institutions such as the Monetary Fund and the World Bank – it is still not possible, but once that will be the case – then the requirement for this is the legal personality of the European Union. The second, very important point is that the Charter of Fundamental Rights is the first to be binding in the text of the Constitution. A third area: Parliament will be strengthened and will be given virtually universal co-decision and co-determination rights. There is a clear commitment to qualified majority voting as the basic principle. In many areas there are improvements, simplification of voting procedures in the Union, but above all there is also some very important progress on the institutional issue, such as sheepmeat and goatmeat. a European Foreign Minister. This will be one of the most exciting issues today and tomorrow, whether the 25 countries and 105 Convention members are ready to put foreign policy under the primacy of qualified majority voting. If that succeeds, I think that's a real breakthrough. I also admit, however, that there are concerns on some issues - for the Germans, for example in the field of immigration policy, for the British or for Sweden in the field of tax policy, for us for example on water resources or on the issue of institutions - and this must be thoroughly discussed in the Intergovernmental Conference, of course with the involvement of the House.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e7597	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! It is true that the enlargement of the European Union is a huge, historic step forward. And the question is: What is the historical character of this step? We must never forget that the establishment of the European Community and then the European Union had a deeper and deeper meaning, namely to heal the wounds which the Second World War had struck in Western Europe and to come across a concept of integration to peace and stability in Europe. This model of European integration has proved absolutely successful for Western Europe over the past five decades. And after the historic opportunity of 1989, the European Union has rightly decided to apply the same approach that has been successful for Western Europe to overcome the division of Europe by the Iron Curtain after 1945, and to apply the same concept, namely integration and cooperation, as an instrument for peace and stability. All of us – all the countries of the European Union that are already members today, and those countries that will join soon – are based on the same hope that the same approach, which has been successful in Western Europe, will now be successful for a larger part of Europe towards East, South-East and Central Europe. Guarantees for success never exist. It always depends on what is actually made of these opportunities. If the last months have also been marked by the fact that the European Union has discussed how to remain functional, if 25 or more countries are members of the European Union, and there have also been decisions, we need to know that we are not moving on a secure empirical basis, but that the agreements that have been reached according to best knowledge and conscience should serve to preserve the European Union functioning. But there is no guarantee for that either. The political will to make this enlargement successful can and must not be achieved with the 1. In May 2004, the challenges will have to be dealt with very decisively. There are, I believe, a number of questions that are of particular interest to us both in Austria, but which are also important geostrategically. With this enlargement of the European Union, we are also experiencing for the first time something that may lead to discussions in many of the new Member States. The European Union is today a highly integrated Community with economic union, with monetary union, with a single market, with a number of competition rules and accompanying policies. All those who have been members of the European Union have been able to take part in this. Of course, all those who are new to it are already rising at a high level of development and have no chance of co-designation in the whole of what has developed in the past. Now we will not be able to turn the wheel of history back and turn back, but we have to draw a conclusion from it in any case: The new Member States must note, as soon as possible, that they also have a say in the further development of the European Union. What does that mean? . That means that in a relatively short period of time, we need to reflect on the development of the European Union so that the new Member States are also rightly given the impression that this is not just a house we are moving into, but it is a common whole that we can actually participate in. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] A second aspect which, as I believe, is essential is the one that Mr Klubobmann Molterer referred to when he said that it should not be enlargement or integration only of the governments, and beyond that of the elites, but also of the parliaments and, I assume, also of the population. I think that there is a good basis for this. I think that the results of the referendums in each of the candidate countries have nevertheless adopted very encouraging levels. So if in the past one has had scepticism whether there will be a majority in Poland, for example, on the basis of a very strong, if one wants to do so, right-wing populist opposition to enlargement, even if there have been doubts about the majority in other countries, then, I must say, the reality has shown that the levels of consent were very high, in almost all states were significantly higher than in the referendums at the time of Austria's accession to the European Union. This is, I believe, a good sign that the peoples of the new Member States are very well aware of what the common objective is and where it is. In the future, we want to be oriented and orientated. This too is a great opportunity to see, because all of these basics that exist today, we will still need to solve the problems ahead of us. And the problems are and remain serious. Of course, we will also have an economic transformation process in the Central and Eastern European countries. It is only necessary to see how high the share of agricultural production in Western Europe is today and how high it remains in the Central and Eastern European countries. There will be a completely fundamental change in the economic structure, with the associated problems. We will have a second problem: because of the welfare gap between today's Member States of the European Union and the new Member States, this new Europe will not only grow larger and more outwardly, but will also increase the competitive pressure within the European Union, because people with similar qualifications in Austria or Germany or in France have quite a different level of income from those in the Czech Republic, Hungary or the Baltic States. You're not supposed to give in to illusions. This will lead to an increase in competitive pressure. On the third hand, there will be a challenge that many Group headquarters will also consider where they will be located in Europe in the future, and, of course, tax competition will also be tightened. I would just like to point out that Slovakia has recently adopted Rule 19/19/19, that is to say: 19 percent VAT, 19 percent Corporate Tax and 19 percent Income Tax. This clearly means that there will also be huge competition at the level of taxation in this enlarged European Union. One could continue the list of these challenges, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. The issue of transport and transit will certainly be discussed today at the urgent request. But we must not be afraid of these challenges. All I say is that we must use the good mood now, both in the Member States of the European Union and in the new acceding countries, to deal with the challenges ahead, because only then will the enlargement of the European Union be a success for all participants. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! There is no doubt as to the accuracy and importance of the European Union enlargement project for Austrian Social Democracy. The SPÖ as a Party of European Attitude, which stands unabated on the project of European integration, will support and contribute to this process. At the same time, however, I would like to say that all of what we have agreed here in the Austrian National Council in a resolution by the parliamentary parties to prepare for the enlargement of the European Union has not yet been settled at 100 percent. In this context, I would like to remind colleagues of the other groups of what we have agreed. In connection with the enlargement of the European Union, we have agreed to carry out a major infrastructure offensive towards the north, east and south-east in order to improve transport links with the candidate countries. – Unfortunately, there has been relatively little progress since we decided on this request. We have agreed that in Austria we need a qualification offensive for Austrian workers, so that it will increase for them due to the increase in the number of employees. Competition pressures in the enlarged European Union will also give better opportunities. – Unfortunately, relatively little has happened in this sector too. We have also agreed to develop specific programmes for the Austrian border regions, so that the transition to the enlarged Europe, as is rightly said today in a newspaper by the mayor of Gmünd, can actually be seen. – Even in these cross-border and border-border projects, there are relatively few specifics to date. That does not mean that everything is lost, that is, if now the enlargement of the European Union on 1st. In May of next year, all that has not yet happened will have to be done in parallel with the enlargement process. It would only be a complete illusion to believe that, if enlargement is being implemented now, one can forget all that – we have really recognized this together – the accompanying music of enlargement should also be in Austria. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and the Greens.]] My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! Let me conclude a political comment. That is why we are also committed in principle to the project of enlargement of the European Union. However, it was equally important for us to take into account the interests of Austria and our people in this enlargement process, just as of course every other country of the European Union does in terms of its own interests and the interests of its own people. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] I believe that, of course, we have benefited from this common Europe and will continue to benefit in the future: in the economic field, in the regional policy field, if I think only of our Objective 1 areas, in the field of quality standards, whether in the production sector or, for example, in the environmental field, we note, when we are on the international road, the difference in this area, whether a country is a member of the European Union or not, but, perhaps, above all, in the field of security policy. It has already been said that at the beginning of this European idea of unification, the goal and the will to stop allowing a major war in Europe between European nations was the aim. Although it has not been possible to think of the 1990s in particular to avoid wars in Europe, it has at least succeeded in ensuring that wars and military conflicts have become impossible between the members of the European Union. This is one of the centuries-old projects that this European agreement has brought to us and to the future. That is why it is also necessary for security reasons to support this project and its enlargement. If I bring some of the candidate countries, such as the Baltic, then I have to say, it is a security signal that former Soviet countries can now become members of this European family for the first time. Then this is a signal that one of the major unsolved security problems, such as that of Cyprus, now has a chance to solve. And it was the right signal of Europe that has been said that we are not going to take part of this divided republic into the European Union, the Community of the European States, but only the entire nation, the total Cypriots, and we are asking for a solution to this problem, where politicians from both sides have rested very comfortably on the status for many years, if I can say so salopp here, and we also have to accept a divided republic long after the end of the Cold War in Europe. Here again, by this enlargement, an opportunity for the population in this region to find a solution. Ladies and gentlemen! But I believe that we will have a lot of homework to do in order to really help all the goals of a functioning united Europe to break through, for example with the institutions. It has already been said that the European Union is far from ready to take account of and be prepared for this round of enlargement. If we imagine that this is still unresolved – although some guidelines have been set in the Constitutional Convention of the European Union, also in the last EU summits, but a solution to this enlargement with regard to decision-making mechanisms as regards internal administrative processes, we have not yet come to the table. I mention only the conditions for enlargement, which are also of particular importance to us and which have also been a condition for our approval of this enlargement process, such as a solution to the free movement of workers. I am very glad that, as a result of a seven-year transitional solution, today we have the opportunity to say to the population: We have taken into account your concerns that enlargement may lead to increased unemployment in Austria. It was important for us to take into account, in the negotiation process, the interests of the Austrian people, for example, in the face of the danger posed by nuclear power plants close to the border and by setting up the milking process, to create a common solution. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] It is necessary for us – and this is an open question – that we should also signal to our people about the question of transit, that we also take account of their concerns and the daily needs, and that we should call for all the liberalisation of transport routes in Europe, and that the European Union should also assume its responsibility when it comes to ensuring the protection of the population from noise and traffic nuisance. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Nor can it be accepted - as there is still no awareness among the representatives of the individual nations in the European Parliament - that the people's representation of the European population is so non-chalant about the concerns of the population of a Member State and goes in the other direction with votes and resolutions. As long as this awareness is not available in the European Parliament, that one must take into account the concerns of all countries, it will be necessary for national parliaments and national governments to take account of the concerns and fears of their own people and to bring them into the institutions during the treaty negotiations and individual discussions – and this also concerns the question of which materials must continue to be subject to the principle of unanimity in the future; this too is an important issue for the future. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Finally, it is also about supporting human rights standards in the European Union. I did not understand that, even in Austria, one considers that it is only because one represents the concerns of a part or of the entire Austrian population that something is blocked, that one therefore signs an anti-European attitude. Ladies and gentlemen! On the contrary! I believe that this common Europe, including the united Europe, the enlarged Europe, will only have a future chance if it is based on the awareness of the population in these countries – not only of institutions, politicians, media opinions and bureaucracy in the institutions and in the countries. We will only create this awareness of Europe if we signal to the people that we are serious about their concerns and wishes that we are also representatives of the people in Europe and towards Europe – not institutional representatives. This task and responsibility we have to face in Austria and as an Austrian in the European Union. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] As I spoke of human rights: In our view, this is not an initiative against a country if we call for its compliance. It cannot be a major problem, for example, if we are discussing the Beneš decrees – an open question, a bilateral question; if you see the European Union as a community of values, then it is also a multilateral question – that a democratic country like the Czech Republic, which has made a very good, very positive development over the last ten, twelve years, draws a conclusion in this area under a dark side of its own past. I believe and hope that, until ratification, there will be a solution in this respect – first signals were positive indeed. Today, with a motion for a resolution, we want to make a strong contribution to the very interests of the Austrian people, the displaced, who, after having participated in fearsome events, have built up this country, ladies and gentlemen. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] I say it again: This is not a threat, it is not an exaggeration of nationalities or country interests, but the natural representation of principles, of values – and this, of course, and above all, includes human rights. [[Abg. May. Mainoni: Human rights!]] [[Abg. May. Mainoni: That's it!]] Ladies and gentlemen! At the end of the security policy – as a former Minister of Defence, I do not want to hide from this, even if my own time has already expired. I believe that the project of a common Foreign and security policy must be forward-looking – without scams, without barriers, but with due consideration and caution, but also consistency. Unfortunately, the European Union is not as far as we would like it to be in this area. Following the experience of the Balkan War, a project of the common security and defence policy was initiated very quickly, with first successes, but we remember only a few weeks ago: In view of the Iraq war, when it came to adopting a single European political, perhaps military, attitude, all the good intentions of a common foreign and security policy were forgotten again. There were two blocks in Europe: the one that, together with the United States of America, carried out the military action in Iraq, and the other that were against it. If we in Europe fail to initiate and establish decision-making mechanisms and common structures at the political and military level, this objective of a common security, defence and foreign policy will remain on paper only. But this is one of these identities: showing solidarity with people in Europe, signaling: If a Member State of the European Union is threatened – no matter who or what – the whole Union becomes, all the states of that Union will come to the aid of the oppressed state and its population, solid and solid. This should be the goal for the future – and that we jointly ensure that when crises arise in our environment or crises break out, the people threatened there will be supported. You can see: The united Europe is an important project, it is a big project, it is a difficult project. We have achieved a great deal in the past, and I believe, especially through critical votes, by rejecting a often unfounded euphoria, we are able to achieve step by step in Austria and in other countries the awareness that a common, united Europe is not a danger but a chance for the future. In this sense, my group also confesses – critically but consistently – to this project of a peaceful, a common, a united Europe.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e7960	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! Many of my previous speakers, in fact all, have pointed this out, and I would like to join in: It is really a special day for Parliament today. The enlargement of the European Union is something – I understand everyone – to which we say with all our heart and where we Members are pleased that we can be with it. Today, this Constitutional Act lays the foundations for ratification in Austria, for EU enlargement. Enlargement is a historic opportunity. It seales the integration of the European continent. The former Communist countries of Central and Eastern Europe are welcome and equal partners in Europe. Every one of us, who once passed the border to a former Communist country and experienced the fate and totalitarian power claim on his own body, must feel joy and satisfaction at the fact that the principles of democracy, freedom, respect for human rights, the rule of law and also the market economy have finally prevailed. Of course, there is a need for adaptation. It is the last and the largest change in the European Union, which has grown from originally six Member States to nine, to twelve and then to 15 countries and is now being extended to 25 members in 2004. This creates an adjustment pressure – my previous speakers have often pointed out – and that will certainly not be easy to deal with. It is the world's largest common economic area of 500 million citizens. This enlargement is particularly important for Austria for various reasons. On the one hand, because, as my clubman has already said, we are moving from the edge to the central position, into the heart of Europe, as was prophetically stated in our Federal Anthem. Of the ten enlargement countries, four are close neighbours of Austria, we have 1 300 kilometres of common border with them. We have a common history, and we often have common ancestors, probably especially in Austria. And we have intensive economic cooperation. I see this differently from you, Mr. Klubobmann Van der Bellen. I do not feel it is small-scale when arguments of economic efficiency and economic space are presented because a healthy economy is the basis for a decent, beautiful life and we all need to work to create these very living grounds for our citizens. That is exactly what we should approve of the countries of Central and Eastern Europe. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Trade with Central and Eastern European countries has increased steadily. Since 1995, Austrian exports to these countries have grown by 12.5 percent on average every year. Direct investment in Eastern Europe is particularly appreciated by Austrian companies: 60 percent of Austrian direct investment goes precisely to these Central and Eastern European countries. Austria is the fifth largest investor in this region. We are in Slovenia and also in Croatia, which you mentioned and which, hopefully, will also be part of the European Union, even the largest investor at all. Of course, there are difficulties in adapting to the labour market. There is a seven-year transitional scheme here. I believe that this provides enough time for the Austrian labour market to adapt. Overall, enlargement has been created by the opening of the East. This fear of job loss is understandable, but largely unfounded. As the Austrian People's Party, we have always understood enlargement as an opportunity, we have always been to do so, and we will be happy and happy today. The Austrians have also taken the original scepticism, and their scepticism has already been turned into acceptance in agreement and by now. While 55 percent of the Austrian population were against enlargement in 1998, 53 percent were already in favour at the beginning of 2001. That's an incredible change. The Austrians have felt that there is an Austrian and a European opportunity here. We have the opportunity to enjoy enlargement, and in the end there is no alternative either. I particularly liked one of the many comments that have been made about enlargement; I would also like to quote from it. Hedwig Kainberger has put the discussion to the fore in the ‘Salzburger Nachrichten’, I believe. Of course, it also drew attention to the risks of this enlargement, but in the end it meant – I would like to quote – that: “When it comes to questions like this, it’s like it’s in life: They are usually better off with self-confidence, courage and fascination for opportunities for new things than those who, in the light of concerns, risk and problems, squeak and frighten back.” I am glad that all four parliamentary parties do not squeak.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e8182	Mrs Mag. Lunacek. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e8200	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! First of all, let me start with a few fundamental comments on this empowerment law and this first step we are taking here in the National Council for enlargement of the EU. For us Greens, this enlargement is indeed the peace project in this Europe. We said that long before Austria joined. For us, it was always clear that Europe is more than this European Union. Of course, this combination also costs money – that has already been said by my clubman Van der Bellen – which is very much needed. But not only does it cost money, but it also takes imagination and courage to let things that are over be over and to go into the future of this common new Europe of the 21st century. ==References== [[Congratulation with the greens.]] It is also about dealing with things together now and not trying to act against each other, as was the case in the 45 years in which the Iron Curtain separated us. This was really not a prosperous time, neither for the people in the East Block nor for the people in our border regions. It is necessary to do things together. From our side there have been long-standing proposals, similar to those agreed between Germany and the Czech Republic in 1997 to write a joint Austro-Czech Declaration, in which each country had the responsibility for the mistakes and for the errors that occurred in the respective country during the Nazi period and thereafter. All these are areas where similar methods are to be chosen. In this context, I would like to remind you of the legal opinion of the international judge Blumenwitz, who says: “The Beneš decrees... are not obsolete. Only those decrees that directly served to carry out the deportation or regulate the settlement of the new population were repealed. All the relevant law-enforcement and ownership are still included in the collection of applicable Czech laws and, according to the Czech case-law, the basis of the New legal order. ... All decrees relating to ownership and state ownership status shall continue to apply and shall be applied if Czech legislation, administration or case law refers to them. Important examples of this “follow-up” are the restitution laws adopted after 1991. According to the decision of the Czech Supreme Court of 29 July, the Court of Justice of the Czech Republic has failed to fulfil its obligations under the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union and the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union on the Functioning of the European Union on the Functioning of the European Union on the Functioning of the European Union of the European Union on the Functioning of the European Union of the European Union on the Functioning of the European Union of the European Union Civil proceedings must be suspended and expropriation procedures concluded under old law.” – Quotes. This is actually an irreconcilable opinion of a recognised international law expert who created it two years ago. This also covers political observation, for for example – understanding its difficult situation of course – I would like to say that – Prime Minister Spidla on 7 May. The "Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung" (Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung) asked what the Beneš decrees are now, saying they are valid and will be valid. In the same place, Foreign Minister Svoboda beat in a radio interview last week, quoted in the "Presse" of the 2nd century. July, where he said: “It’s about the treatment of people during the deportation. No one has said that the deportation itself would be unacceptable.” In this direction, those efforts – as you know, Klaus and Spidla have commissioned a new Constitutional Novel – to prevent “wrong legal interpretations” in connection with restitution issues and the Beneš decrees. That meant that any legal opinion different from the Beneš decrees would be unconstitutional. I therefore note: As it is so far, the Beneš decrees are not dead right, but they are living wrong! However, other signals are opposed, which are quite acceptable. I also note that what Spidla said at the European Forum a week ago is a first step in the right direction and that everything that continues in this direction is to be supported. I also note here: There are no opponents, no hostilities between peoples that are not revisable. The history of the Germans and the Czechs has been extremely successful for more than 900 years by far and over long distances, and so it must be again. ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e9316	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The fact that the forthcoming EU enlargement in the economic field is a major challenge, but also a major change, is clear to most people. How strong this change will be can be seen, for example, by the fact that today we have an export share of 60 percent to the EU countries, which will be about three quarters in the future. Without a doubt, our neighbours – Poland as a Central European country – have a major share in this. The least aware of the fact that the export share, which goes only to these five countries – Poland, the Czech Republic, Slovakia, Hungary and Slovenia – is almost twice as large as that in all BENELUX and Nordic countries within the European Union. That is to say, the Netherlands, Belgium, Luxembourg, Finland, Sweden and Denmark do not have much more than half the export share of these new EU countries in Central Europe. When you use a global comparison, it actually becomes so dramatic: These five countries have a third higher export share than the countries America, Russia, China, Japan and India combined. This clearly shows how important neighborhood is in economic areas as well. Nevertheless, the economic rail is actually only a side-by-side matter. – I myself grew up on the border in a house that was located just a few hundred meters from the Iron Curtain of that time. I have experienced almost weekly that there were not only barbed wire fences visible there and mine fields were laid, but also how to look for refugees during the night with light-purple ammunition. No week has passed without shooting on the other side. I therefore believe that we must look at this aspect of peace in the foreground. In my opinion, this is not only the end of the division of Europe that has been decided in Yalta, but also a more than one hundred years of confrontation is ended, a confrontation – for whatever reason – whether the national, dynastic, religious, cultural or economic moments that have played there have been overcome. It is now the first time that we are going into a phase of constant cooperation, and this is not just about a phase of a few years, but about permanent, lasting cooperation. Europe is gaining stability, through the stabilisation of Central Europe, but also in the form that geostrategically almost the entire Baltic Sea region belongs to the EU in the future, and that at the same time two very important hubs in the Mediterranean – Cyprus, on the one hand, with all its meaning for the Middle East, and on the other, Malta, with an unestimated significance for the North African region, are coming to the EU. What it is all about is that we have to try to implement and use these opportunities accordingly. There is no doubt that Europe's decision-making capacity is being built up at the same time, so that enlargement alone not only strengthens quantities but also deepens it internally. There needs to be a change – and I believe that this must be a matter of course. However, it seems decisive to me that this is not the aspect that is in the foreground, but that we deal with what should happen in the future. There is no doubt that there is still a need for recovery in many areas. There are intense relationships with many of these countries, but we are all required – especially in the coming times – to intensify and improve these relations. The working group was then not established in the Federal Chancellery, but in the Social Ministry. Now, however, this working group receives the mandate of the Federal Government, and I think it is an important point that this subject does not, as it were, remain in a department. In our view, it is very important that the legal involvement of the Federal Chancellor's Office's Constitutional Service is also ensured. Legal anchorage is urgently necessary so that the framework conditions can also be enforced at the legal level. High house! There is already a gender equality law in Germany. The cornerstones we want to put in the parliamentary debate and debate must go in the direction that, where necessary, we are working out sanctions to raise awareness at the beginning of our actions – including our legal measures – and that we are offensively committed in our society to the recognition of disabled people as equal. It is essential that we define these changed framework conditions by law, which then apply not only in this year, but for the whole century. It is also important, in my opinion, that there should be an association action for disabled people's organisations so that they can more effectively enforce the interests of disabled people. It is also crucial that we can use existing authorities for the process so that these authorities, which already have training in dealing with disabled people, such as the Federal Social Office, can intervene and can be contact persons and fighters for disabled people. ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e11848	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen, On the comments of Mrs Mag. I would just like to say the following about the blind judge: As my colleague Elisabeth Scheucher-Pichler tells me, this blind judge is now employed at the Jugendamt in Klagenfurt. So there are probably people who give these people a chance and hire them. The social situation of disabled people has changed considerably in Austria in recent years. Prejudices and mutual resentments have been reduced, and disabled people have also become visible people in everyday life and on the streets of “invisible citizens”. There has been a paradigm change in disability policy in recent decades: Away from the disabled as an object of care to a self-determined disability movement – that is something that is very important for my concepts – away from the well-to-do demise to the equal with each other, away from the exception and hiding to integration in all social areas and to the presence in public space, away from counter-productive protection provisions to equality and human rights. This puts forward new tasks for policies made by people with disabilities and for people with disabilities. People with disabilities are experts and, as such, should also be involved in politics and politics in shaping their living conditions, as my previous speaker, Mrs Haidlmayr, has already called for. Many former provisions on the protection of disabled people are now regarded as discriminatory by those concerned. For example, a blind person, even if legally formed, may not legally sign a contract alone. On the occasion of the European Year of People with Disabilities, the Austrian Federal Government has a comprehensive package of measures focusing on: Accessibility, integration in the workplace and the media, which will permanently improve the living situation of disabled people in Austria. The Austrian Federal Government will remove barriers and discrimination in the minds and in the laws, i.e. a law on disability equalisation, which includes ensuring the accessibility of re- and new buildings throughout the public sector, including public transport and transport areas, accessibility-free access to e-government and e-learning, as well as the recognition of sign language. I want to make a swivel for the care money: I think the care allowance is one of the most important achievements of recent years. There is a great desire on the part of the disabled to take care and their flexible organization into their own hands in the sense of a self-determined life. I consider it extremely important that those concerned receive the care allowance as cash benefits and thus who can decide when, where, and how to care for them. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] The people with disabilities are concerned with living in spite of their need for care. For many of these disabled people, the current care allowance is not sufficient to be able to pay sufficient assistance. Even today, young disabled people are still accommodated in the old age home. As a result – this is simply an incentive on my part – it needs an open level of care with hourly accounting. The preparation of a draft law on the equalisation of the disabled by the establishment of the working group of the Federal Government with the involvement of the experts concerned, the offices and the Constitutional Service of the Federal Chancellor's Office is expected to take place this year. Based on this draft, the Federal Government is expected to forward to Parliament by the end of this year a government template for a disability equalisation law. I call on all of us to actively support this objective! Thank you.

In order to show that this committee is working productively, I would like to present a concrete example here in the plenary session: the training concept in the Federal Ministry of the Interior. The Court of Auditors has identified four shortcomings. He criticises the fact that there is a separate basic training between the federal security guard and the federal army, that the executive service operates different courses and that there is a variety of training facilities and little efficiency in this use. The Court of Auditors has now proposed reducing training facilities and saving € 2 million annually. The training of senior officials also takes place in joint training courses, but in the selection procedure only the professional qualification has been tested, but the personal suitability is not unfortunately. And decentralized vocational training has caused an additional burden in procurement. The Court's recommendations, such as the harmonisation of course structures, the reduction of training facilities and the increased review of personal fitness, have been included. Federal Minister Strasser has already implemented this very consistently: There is a reform concept for training, 40 percent personnel savings in the training area, only more than 10 instead of 16 locations in the field of training; and – the most important project! – According to the President of the Court of Auditors, the pooling of police and Gendarmerie will bring further savings between €75 million and €150 million. All in all, I believe that the Court of Auditors has fulfilled all the best on its behalf. Thankfully, President Fiedler has already noted that he has a budgetary basis in the future. There are, of course, still unfulfilled desires, such as one’s own right of service, but one will remain in conversation about them. The Court of Auditors is an instrument which calls for economy and gentle handling of taxpayers' money. We need to be more efficient and better in the public sector. And the Court of Auditors is a guarantee that we will continue to have an independent and effective audit instrument in the future. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e12827	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The topic of transit currently has a central position in Austrian politics. Even if it is overlaid by other day-to-day policy issues, it is precisely because of its sustainability and its potential negative impact for Austria as a whole that is certainly the central issue here in the house. The transit traffic, and this is also fact, has only a small share of total Austrian transport, namely about 8 per cent, however, ladies and gentlemen, individual corridors are extremely burdened by transit traffic. For example, on the Brenner the proportion of transit traffic is more than 90 percent, and especially the geographical location, but above all the topographic position, the mountain valleys, make the situation unbearable to the population living there. Ladies and gentlemen! Our solidarity is needed if people can no longer sleep in these transit-loaded valleys, are exposed to physical stress and suffer health damage. [[Congratulations with the liberals and the ÖVP.]] I already know that the Brenner Pass and also the Tauernautobahn-Scheitelbahn are relatively far away from Vienna and much further away from Brussels, but it is a question of solidarity to help people there. We need to protect the people who live in these valleys. To illustrate just one example of how dramatic the situation is there: If one of these transit valleys produces a noise load of 71 dB caused by truck freight traffic, a noise load of 51 dB is still recorded at 1.1 kilometres away and at a height difference of 300 metres. They see that by this funnel effect, because of the particular topographical situation there is a very, very dramatic situation for the population living there. And that also makes the difference to other traffic-loaded locations. Nature landscapes with the people living there are affected and destroyed by the truck transit. We cannot and must not stand by, ladies and gentlemen. [[General condolences.]] I am very grateful, especially for the applause of the opposition, because I believe that this is a matter in which we should try to choose a common procedure, because this common procedure is in demand in Brussels. Now let me talk to you for details. A first detail: the traffic forecasts. – Studies show that all road freight transport in Austria will increase by 50 per cent without any measures, i.e. if it really were to roll freely from 2004 onwards, and will increase by a total of 70 per cent by the year 2010, with 35 per cent due to the accepted liberalisation of cross-border road freight transport from Central and Eastern European countries – and this must also be said to be due to this. That means: The current problem and theme is the North-South transit, but in future Austria is also an extremely important issue for East-West transit. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] This particular situation in Austria was already taken into account in 1993. At the time, the European Union granted our country a transit solution. At that time the difficulty was already recognized, and this is important in the legal context to this day and up to our demands in the future. However, at that time – and this criticism must be made – the transit agreement did not provide for a pass that would automatically have provided for a transitional regime if the purpose of the transit contract were not met. And, ladies and gentlemen, the goal of the transit agreement has not been achieved. The original objective was to reduce the pollutant percentage by 60 percent under the 1991 pollutant scheme. This has not happened, and that is to say, the effects of transit are more serious than was assumed at the time. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Another statistical figure, which shows how dramatic Austria is affected by this transit traffic, is affected by the cross-border transport of goods: Of the European states that lie along the Alps, i.e. Switzerland, France and Austria, Austria has the highest traffic burden. Some figures – rail and road together –: Switzerland's traffic burden is 29 million tonnes a year, the traffic burden of France's 51 million tonnes, the transport burden of Austria compared to that of 93 million tonnes! This shows how much Austria is burdened by this transit traffic. In particular, Austria cuts off extremely badly at the road. In addition, a few figures: The share of the road in the modal split of cross-country transit traffic in Switzerland is 8.9 million tonnes – is very, very small there – in France 40.8 million tonnes, and Austria is again top riders with 61.1 million tonnes, ladies and gentlemen. These figures alone demonstrate the importance and urgency of a future regime. [[Congratulations on Members of all political groups.]] This disproportionately high transport load in Austria has a particularly serious impact on the population and, above all, on the environment. It is pointed out – through the Alpine Convention, and it is also legally anchored – that Austria is one of the sensitive Alpine zones in particular. Studies prove that the same traffic – and this is very important! – with the same pollutant emission in the flatland, only one third of the NOx concentration caused by it in the mountain valleys. In other words, with the same pollutant emission, three times as much NOx concentration in the mountain valleys like on the flatland! This too is another proof that there is an urgent need for action here. On the impact on Europe – because it is always the case of the single entry of Austria, of the solitaire situation, as it always says: It is very important for Europe as a whole to have something to do with this, because road transport has undisputed negative effects not only in Austria but throughout Europe. Alpine eco-systems are particularly sensitive to air pollutants and pollutant deposits. An ecologically intact alpine region – and this has been dramatically exposed to us in recent years – protects us from avalanches, murals, and from erosions. It is an area of pan-European importance in topography, climate, water, vegetation, wildlife, landscape and culture, and any impairment of this function can lead to disastrous consequences for the pan-European area. Ladies and gentlemen! The economic impact is also not to be overlooked. It is a real economic problem, which has developed here, since, due to the high levels of pollution in certain regions, no more operations can be carried out. According to a balance sheet available to me, the external costs caused by road freight transport, namely 7 percent of EU gross domestic product, exceed the benefits it derives, which is only 6 percent of EU GDP. This is evidenced by a 1999 WHO study, which is not just some statistics that may be beautiful. Much more important than the economic impact, ladies and gentlemen – the Federal Minister has mentioned it very often – are the health effects. The same 1999 WHO study shows the dramatic consequences for Austria caused by air pollution alone by road transport every year: 2 400 premature deaths, 2 700 additional cases of chronic bronchitis in adults, 20,000 additional cases of chronic bronchitis in children under 15 years of age, 15,000 additional asthma attacks in children and over 1.3 million additional days of illness! – Ladies and gentlemen, not an Austrian study to prove how dramatic the situation is, but a 1999 WHO report. And now to the legal situation, ladies and gentlemen of the House. – Austria was granted two things primarily by the Commission and the European Union on the occasion of the 1995 Accession Treaty, and this is also important for our further negotiations: firstly, the permanent reduction in emissions by 60 percent – since 1991 until the expiry date – which is known to have not been achieved, and secondly, that European framework conditions will be in force until the Treaty expire, ensuring a sustainable transport policy for Europe as a whole. This has not yet been done. These are the EU's primary commitments to Austria on the occasion of the 1995 Accession Treaty. Unfortunately, these commitments are still not fulfilled. Even the European Commission, very much appreciated Ladies and gentlemen, in its report, which was drawn up with the support of the European Environment Agency, it concludes that the target set out in Protocol 9 has not been achieved, i.e. the 60 % reduction in pollutant. – The European Commission itself thus comes to this conclusion, and a supplementary study by the European Environment Agency also comes to this conclusion. Therefore, from my point of view, Austria is now legally in the situation of getting the right to a new regime, but in the actual situation, it is necessary to fight for a transitional regime. ==References== The current Ecopoints Regulation, as set out in Protocol 9 to the Treaty of Accession to the European Union, expires on December this year. The aim of this scheme – I repeat it here because it is so important – was and is a permanent and sustainable reduction of pollutant to a level of 60 percent of the 1991 baseline. This goal, ladies and gentlemen, has not yet been achieved. Even the Austrian Road Costs Directive, promised in 1994, with the Declaration No 34 on the Accession Protocol, still does not exist. Ladies and gentlemen, here we are not decent, but the European Union is primarily legal. [[Congratulations on Members of the Freeman and the SPÖ.]] However, I would also like to talk about the pan-European benefits of the Ecopoints regime, since I am constantly listening to voices that this ecopoints regulation may be a barrier to lobbys in the transit area across Europe. Here, too, the balance sheet of this ecopoints regulation gives right. The current ecopoint system, with its objective of reducing the noise and pollutant emissions caused by truck transport, has a positive effect across Europe, as a result of which fleet renewal of freighters, transport operators and freight forwarders has been achieved, and secondly, the shift to rail has been further stimulated and, thirdly, of course, it also benefits from the forced movement of combined transport across Europe. Unfortunately, since it has not yet been possible at European level to create the necessary European framework conditions for sustainable transport policy – I have already implemented it – it is necessary to continue an effective transitional regime in order to ensure that there is no progress towards a genuine sustainable transport system. Ladies and gentlemen! In fact, it would be a step backwards if nothing had been done now, so if this scheme had been replaced by the end of this year. It would not be possible to achieve the objective of reducing pollutants, but rather to achieve the opposite effect, namely a leaping increase in pollutant emissions, would be the logical consequence. With regard to the need for a transitional regime, ladies and gentlemen, in the course of the negotiations on such a scheme following the existing ecopoints system, Austria has always stressed that, in view of the forthcoming enlargement of the European Union, it is also after the 1st century. In January 2004 specific long-term solutions must be in force at European level. There will also be East-West transit on us, and it will require an Austrian rule, ladies and gentlemen, to do so. Negotiations on the transitional arrangements are only more in the conciliation procedure before the start of the third reading. I then come to vote separately on 3rd reading. To speak on July. The results of the second reading were absolutely unsatisfactory. The European Parliament, in its considerations, points out in detail, inter alia, with regard to the Alpine Convention, that loads and risks in the field of inland alpine and alpine transport should be reduced to a level which is acceptable to humans, animals and plants and their habitats, but the measures proposed by the European Parliament do not take account of this objective at all. As a result, there is evidence that there are some in the European Parliament who believe that these objectives must not be pursued, but, quite the contrary, that they must ensure free movement through Austria. [[In the case of the liberals as well as the aggregator, I should like to thank you for your support. May. Worm.]] High house! The performance of Austria in connection with its accession to the European Union and the associated Treaties shall be indicated. Austria has already made many progress towards a long-term and sustainable European transport policy. They will all know them, but I would like to point out once again, because it is also important in future negotiations to have this in subconsciousness. First: Our obligations under Protocol 9 in the railway sector have been fully fulfilled. Since the transit contract in-force, Austria has spent €800 million on combined transport alone. In addition, Austria has made a number of investments during this period, particularly with regard to the expansion of railway infrastructure, the increase in the attractiveness of rail and combined transport. For example, the transit-related rail expansion Brenner – Tauern – Schober – Semmering – Wechsel – was driven forward – Danube. A total of €8.3 billion has been invested since 1995, of which in the years 2000 to 2002 alone – and in this place a praise for the government! – €4 billion. For the next ten years, ladies and gentlemen, Austria is planning further investments in the main rail transit axes to a total of €11 billion. In addition, Austria’s express willingness – you know – to make its contribution to the construction of the Brenner Base Tunnel, which is expected to be around €5 billion, is estimated to cost, or will cost more, if 20 percent co-financing is guaranteed by the European Union and above all the possibility of effective cross-financing. I can still remember very well when the Italian Minister of Transport on 1st October. This year we visited the house and our Minister for Transport and Infrastructure succeeded in signing a contract that at least points the way there. Ladies and gentlemen! But also road development is one of the areas in which we have fulfilled our tasks. Over the next ten years, we will invest over 6 billion in road construction, which is necessary. However, and this is also important: It would have been an important, great signal if all the parties represented in Austria in the European Parliament had agreed here. This means that after the expiry of the transit contract, that is, after ten years, at the end of 2003, one cannot pretend to have never been, and free ride ahead. It cannot be that the achieved goal can then be undermined, undermined and then ad absurdum. These two objectives have thus been agreed, and this is not only a moral right, but also primary law, as lawyers have already explained and as it also confirms opinions and proceedings. At the time, however, it was rejected – I would like to mention that without any debt allocations; but it is simply important for the facts to know and explain – to address the road cost directive that was promised at the time. That is why the time limit of ten years in the transit contract has been promised by the EU to create the Road Costs Directive, which will also allow goods traffic to be shifted from road to rail. It was said that this directive would be in force in ten years' time, so that the transit agreement would no longer be needed. It has failed to take into account the event: What happens if the transit contract expires at the end of 2003 and the Road Costs Directive is not yet in force as an instrument for regulating transit traffic? – A sentence would probably have been sufficient, for example: If the road cost guidelines are not yet in force, the transit contract automatically extends by one year. – Then we would not have all been dealing with this problem, which I have been working with for months, since I took office, and for which I have acted intensively in the interests of Austria in Brussels and everywhere in Europe. But this sentence does not exist, and the fact is that, of course, some Member States are taking advantage of the time pressure because they know that if you don't agree – that is a good position from the point of view of the others – if you don't agree on a compromise, then the transit contract expires, and the free movement of goods is then opened in the truest sense of the word door and gate. [[Congratulations with the liberals and the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! If you think about these figures, you also better understand that Austria does not want to “brown an extrudurst”, as a political politician once said, but Austria has a right to what has been promised, namely a sustainable reduction of pollutant by 60 percent and a limit on the number of transiting trucks. Ladies and gentlemen! Of course, it would also be interesting to know the following – I am glad that Mr Mainoni has mentioned the figures on the volume of transport here –: What is the overall volume of transport compared to rail/road, because it is also recognized – even without any credit allocations; I am trying to pursue a really detideologicalized transport policy – that we have a huge need for recovery, especially as regards rail, especially in cross-border freight transport. Among the 90 million tonnes of goods you mention, Mr. Mainoni, which dates back to 2000 – which are transported annually over Austria's Alpine arc – we need to know that 60 million tonnes of them, two thirds of them, are transported on the road and 30 million tonnes on the rail. The ratio is two thirds of the road, one third of the rail. The 50 million tonnes of transport in France – according to a study by the Bern Spatial Planning Institute – and this study is neutral and undisputed – are in such a way that 40 million tonnes of transport are carried out on the road and 10 million tonnes are carried out on the rails. So also a bad relationship, even worse than in Austria. In this connection to the figures of Switzerland – one forgives me when I repeatedly strata Switzerland as an example, but I do not do so because I am Vorarlberger and Vorarlberg adjoins Switzerland, but because it is an interesting comparison: In the case of 30 million tonnes of transport, which goes over the Swiss Alpine arc each year, the ratio is exactly the other way round: Of the 30 million, 20 million tonnes are transported on the track and 10 million tons on the road, just the other way around Austria. With this, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to point out that we should have a common interest in clarifying the need for resurgence, which leads to cross-border freight transport in the Alpine Arches, or to clarify it together. And I think the main focus will probably have to be on the Brenner Base Tunnel. I am also glad that we succeeded in bringing the Brenner Base Tunnel with an investment volume of € 4.5 to €5 billion to the so-called TENs, to the Trans-European Networks. As you know, these are particularly supported by the EU, with 50 percent of the planning costs. and now with up to 20 percent – contrary to previous intentions, according to which there should be up to 10 percent – of investment costs. This is a success, as I am delighted that more than 100 projects submitted for these major European corridors will be proposed in total 18, as a result of the Van Miert Group, this “high-level group”. Of these 18 proposed projects, two are really completely undisputed: the Galileo satellite system and the coastal road, that is motorways. For the other 16 projects, there are five Austrian projects: either 100 percent or so we are so touched that we can be very satisfied with this, but this is an investment volume of € 14 billion. TEN projects in Austria will be co-financed by the EU, and half of the planning costs will be co-financed, if it remains as it is now proposed. This brings us forward, it is true that the Austrian general transport plan, which has already been decided, is very much in line with the development in Europe. The transit problem has already been covered in detail by my previous speakers. This is certainly a subject – that has already been said – that should find enough space in this House and must, because it concerns us all. This topic must concern every Austrian and every Austrian, because our habitat, our environment and thus our future depend on it. Besides Tyrol and Salzburg, which are very often referred to here as truly massively affected countries, Carinthia is also greatly affected by the transit problem. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Wattaul.]] Mr Wattaul! Of course, Lower Austria, Vienna and Upper Austria are also affected. The transit problem can be seen as a whole. But I believe that Tyrol, Salzburg, Carinthia and also Vorarlberg have special problems due to the topography. Close valleys and special climatic conditions also contribute to the additional and negative impact of pollutant emissions and noise in these regions. However, I am glad that the first black and blue-black government has already sent a general traffic plan on the journey, so to speak, which has made a real change, and in which we have known that changes must be made both on the road and in rail transport, and that there must be massive investment in order to lay a foundation for Austria's failure to meet in this area. As a Carinthian MP, I am now thinking mainly of the full extension of the tomb tunnel in the packing section, of the extension line between the packsattel and Moderniach or of the full connection of Villach or also to the second Katschberg and Tauern tunnel tube. With this needlehole on this scalding line there has been for years, if not decades of urgent need for something to happen. [[Abg. Dr. Lichtenberger: Shouldn't you prefer to expand the track?]] As far as rail transport is concerned, I am thinking in particular of the extension of the coral track, which is certainly very important especially for the area in the east. But I am also thinking about the expansion of the Tauernbahn. There there is a two-track extension, which in turn closes a needle tube. Ladies and gentlemen! This, in turn, is a sign – and this is important – that of course we consider the track to be important. However, we will only be able to take advantage of the opportunities and opportunities in the future if there is a double-track expansion. In addition, countless stations are renewed. A great deal of money will also be invested in noise protection measures, both in road and rail development. Noise is particularly distressing, so noise protection is especially important to them. For example, a motorway was built in the Liesertal over 20 years ago, which would no longer be built today. That is why we owe it to the population of this language to ensure that good noise protection measures and facilities will at least make the suffering it has been added to it bearable. Something else is important in this context: We have laid the foundation for the fact that, especially by means of appropriate tendering procedures, I have often said this here and I would very much like to repeat it once again – even small and medium-sized enterprises in the regions at last have the opportunity to participate in such a large project. The times are over when only large companies or corporations can build a bridge or road. We have now achieved that regional companies and construction companies with 20, 30 or 100 employees have a chance to participate in such projects. We are thus strengthening the economy, the location of our country and thus our home country! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Ladies and gentlemen! However, transport policy is not only an important issue, but also a very small one. I would be allowed to go back to a subject that I have already mentioned sometime, because I believe it is important. We have in Carinthia, in the southernmost federal state, which is very often due to various – often only politically motivated – reasons in all mouths, also traffic technical problems. That is why I would like to make one more point that we must take a very clear stand against the double toe. I would like to place here that Carinthia, especially as a holiday country and also from the economic point of view, has the problem of having to pay double toad. This is certainly not easy for Carinthia, and something should happen in this regard. I think it's very important that the exchange lock is preserved. I mean, this is a promise we have to make here in this House for Carinthia, but also for Salzburg. The barn lock is a tourist facility that we urgently need, a facility that hundreds of commuters use every day. In addition, I remember the terrible disaster in the Tauern tunnel. If at that time we had not had this lock, which has often been said, ladies and gentlemen, then we would have experienced a traffic collapse every weekend, but it could be turned off by the elusive routes Tauernschluuse and Felbertauern. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Finally, another problem in this context – for everyone else, I hope it's just a Carinthian problem, but for us it's not easy –: The evening flights were deleted from us. That is to say, we no longer have a flight from Vienna to Klagenfurt in the evening; on the one hand, this is not quite easy for us parliamentarians, on the other hand, it is certainly not easy for many economic operators who need to return to Vienna every day or very often by plane, but after 20 o'clock. These are areas which should perhaps not be overlaid by the pan-European transit problem, but I think that the urgent request and this day should also be useful in pointing out these problems, because we will be able to proceed a little further by solving such problems.

I also say this as a Tyrolean, there are other people who are disadvantaged by these burdens. We also want to make transport more environmentally compatible and acceptable through the new Road Costs Directive. [[Demonstrativ accompaniment of the Abg. Dr. Jarolim.]] We are still of the opinion that this is the more appropriate way of doing things, especially because even if an insurance comes in and pays off the insurance amount in case of a theft, the work of art can no longer be obtained. That is, the amount of insurance that replaces the work of art is not a real replacement. We therefore believe that the funds spent on insurance premiums should be better used for the use and expansion of the security facilities in the museums. ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e18466	Mr President. High house! The report that is being discussed today is, in fact, only a partial report. It contains only that part, which is the subject of the average income of the population as a whole, and it does not contain that part that should deal with how many people and which persons receive more than 80 000 S – now 5 887,87 € – 14 times a year. The fact that this report is only a partial report was presented in detail by my previous speakers, including the circumstances that led to it, and the reasons for it. They are, in short, seeking that many public bodies dispute that the transmission of these data, i.e. the remuneration or the names of service providers, can be brought into line with the data protection requirements of the Court of Auditors, and have ultimately led the Court of Auditors to call on the Constitutional Court in view of these refusals. On the other hand, in December 2000, the Constitutional Court asked the European Court of Justice for a preliminary ruling in the cases pending by the Court of Auditors and asked whether the publication of data – on the one hand, the name, on the other hand, of those persons – was compatible with Community law. The European Court of Justice took a decision in May of this year and I was asked to present the final position on this matter to the House. The European Court of Justice has held that it does not wish to decide on the matter itself but refers it back to the Austrian Constitutional Court. It has considered that it is quite possible to publish data with Community law and has developed the following legal code which I would like to read to you in its text. This rule of law reads as follows: "The transmission of income data by workers of public bodies for the purpose of publication in an annual report may be compatible with Community law. It is necessary to ensure that the disclosure is necessary and appropriate in order to achieve the objective of proper administration of public funds. It is up to the national courts to examine whether the disclosure of the names is necessary or whether an anonymous transmission of the data is sufficient.” – As far as the European Court of Justice is concerned. It is now up to the Constitutional Court to make the decision itself. I assume that the European Court of Justice has now made a statement on this matter to the extent that the Constitutional Court will decide this year. I am very happy when a decision of this kind can be made at the same time, because this can make a highly unsatisfactory situation out of the world, because – and this has already been addressed by some previous speakers – it is unsatisfactory for all of us that public bodies ignore, ignore or at least develop constitutional concerns as to whether this law can be implemented at all. I believe that this is a situation that cannot be in the interests of the Court of Auditors or legal certainty or even of the National Council. Thank you.

Because of this imbalance, it took a long time for this agreement to be concluded. We will, of course, agree with this agreement, but it also includes areas such as political dialogue. I would also like to draw attention to cooperation in the social and cultural field, so that everything is very necessary and meaningful. In our view, however, it would have been necessary to negotiate this free trade agreement not only with the Republic of South Africa, but with all the countries of the South African Customs Union, because it is now, as it were, an imbalance for these countries. The EU will also be able to export to these countries, but they do not have the same opportunities towards the EU. To list only a few countries: Botswana, Lesotho, Namibia and Swaziland. But I would like to conclude with the motto: What's not, can still be! – Perhaps there will be an extension in the future, which would undoubtedly be useful for the whole region of South Africa – and not only for the Republic of South Africa, which has always had an important economic importance in this region. In order to achieve a balance between countries, it would be necessary to continue working in this direction from the EU side. – We will also agree with the other templates. Thank you.

We do not forgive them according to the principle of foundry, but those who make a really good priority request good preferential professorships, are also replaced by these personnel costs. This is the competition that takes place among the universities. I mean, if you have a slightly tighter budget this year and next year a very good budget, without any bonds, without deposits, then you can expect the modern managers of modern universities to explain their plans by the end of the next year in accordance with the University Law 2002; they can continue to plan, because the budgets are also set in the law for the two following budget years. I understand this with forward-looking management – no more and no less.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-09-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00028_d7e21871	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! There is more time to reach the target for those they need, because the results of the last student survey and the ongoing evaluation have shown that such improvements are necessary. Accordingly, the duration of the entitlement to study allowance for disabled students will be extended by up to half of the planned study period. For students with children who have higher costs compared to other students for housing and childcare, the aid will be increased. 1 600 students will benefit from it; the woman minister mentioned it. In addition to the state support measures, the aim is to improve the specific study conditions. Additional assistance should be provided through the creation of suitable childcare facilities, as they already exist at some universities, an intensification of e-learning components and increased information on existing support offers will also be sought. But I think it will change a lot of things with good will from all sides and hopefully move too. Thank you.

If you read the report, you will find the following – I quote literally – in it: “In the case of students with low social levels, parental allowances represent 16% of the total budget, whereas in the case of high-level students almost half”, 50 percent. On the other hand, the study aid is: “3 per cent for high-level students” and 20 per cent for low-level students. If you want to tell me now that 50 percent is being compensated for by 20 percent of study aid, I wonder how the Department of Education's accounting department comes to this wonderful result. [[Congratulations with the Greens and representatives of the SPÖ.]] The last point in the report seems to me to be remarkable. You just need to read, then you'll find the data. The impact of the extension of the study aid after the introduction of the study fees was noted. An analysis of social origin has shown that students from high-level and high-level levels in particular have benefited, as the students from low-level shifts have already received the money. – In other words, if you had taken the right action, you would have extended the existing study grants, which would have helped them from lower social levels. The enlargements you have carried out, not in terms of height, but in relation to the circle of reference, have not taken social action. They have already socially favoured students for more money, which is also a good act, but it does not benefit those who would have needed it. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] My final remark: As part of the PISA study, it has been tried to examine how young people can interpret data at the end of compulsory education, how they can interpret tables, among other things. I can tell you: If the Austrian young people had interpreted the tables as you would the report on the situation of the students, we would have been far behind Germany, according to PISA study, and then it would be nothing with the predicate “world class” that you put at the schools.
